This is consistent with the sūrah's earlier instruction to travel and look at how creation begins, and with its assumption that the evidence of the world is available to anyone willing to examine it.

The qualifier also tells the reader what kind of response the sign is meant to produce. A ruin invites an inference rather than a feeling, and the Book addresses people who can follow an argument from an effect to its cause, which is the same capacity it appeals to when it points at the heavens and the earth.

**What Was Actually Left Behind**

Commentators offer three answers to the question of what the sign consists of: the ruins of the town, the stones or the rain that came down upon the people, or the account of their story as it is preserved in revelation. The third reading has the widest reach, since ruins erode and stones are scattered while the narrative travels across the world in every language into which the Book is translated. The two readings are not in competition, and the Book's phrasing accommodates both: a physical trace that was there for the first audience and a reported history that performs the same function for everyone who comes later. What the modern reader encounters is the second form of the sign.

The inventory is kept short, which suits the argument. What remains is not the wealth the town accumulated or the institutions it built but built space itself, and the Book's descriptions of ruined peoples regularly reduce a civilization to its walls in order to show what its people had been relying on.

**Why the Judgment Outlives the Condemned**

Leaving a ruin in place is an act that postpones the closing of a file, and the Book's purpose in doing it is stated in the same sentence: the remains are for people of understanding, which is to say for people who have not yet made their decision. If the only function of the punishment were retribution, the site could have been erased. What the sentence describes is a warning that continues to work after the crime has been answered, and the design of that arrangement is explicitly instructive rather than vindictive. The sūrah's own audience is the beneficiary. A community in Makkah that had not yet been destroyed was being told why a ruined town on the northern road had never been rebuilt.

**A Sign Whose Meaning Had To Be Recorded**

A ruin is a poor witness on its own. Walls do not announce whether a town was abandoned because its wells failed, because a trade route shifted, or because a fire swept through, and travelers in every century have supplied their own explanations for the empty settlements they passed. The sign this verse mentions therefore required an interpreter, and the Book supplies the interpretation in the same narrative that records the destruction. That is the practical function of the accounts collected in this chapter: they convert a landscape into a testimony by attaching a cause to an outcome. For the reader who never visits the site, the account is the sign, and its clarity does not depend on archaeological reconstruction. What was preserved for people of understanding was preserved twice, in the ground and in the text, and the second preservation is the one that reaches everyone.


The Book repeatedly directs its listeners toward the ruins of earlier nations, and the sūrah's later verses will say that the dwellings of ʿĀd and Thamūd are evident to the Meccans as they travel. Elsewhere the Book asks whether its audience does not pass by the remains of the people of Lot in the morning and at night, and it treats their failure to reflect on what they see as the strangest part of the whole matter (37:137–138). The instruction is not to feel sorrow at a pile of stones; it is to connect the stones to a warning that is currently in circulation. A ruin becomes a lesson only when someone standing in front of it asks what produced the emptiness, and the Book assumes that the question is within reach of any traveler with a functioning mind.

**Ruins That Stand Beside Blind Hearts**

The Book pairs the physical ruin with an inner blindness, and the pairing sharpens what this verse means by people of understanding. One passage describes the societies destroyed for persisting in wrongdoing, leaving total ruin behind them along with abandoned wells and lofty palaces, and then asks whether the readers have not travelled so that their hearts may reason and their ears may listen, adding that it is not the eyes that go blind but the hearts in the chests (22:45–46). The sign of this verse is therefore addressed to an organ that a journey cannot repair by itself. A traveler can stand in front of an abandoned town and see only broken masonry, and the Book treats that outcome as a condition of the heart rather than a shortage of evidence.

**A Sign That Does Not Force Its Conclusion**

Throughout these verses the Book's evidence has one consistent property: it is available and it does not compel. Creation is offered as a sign to those who travel and look; the ruins are offered as a lesson to those who understand; the account of the towns is offered to those who hear. Nothing in the arrangement overrides a person's ability to pass by without asking the question. That is what makes the phrase in this verse a criterion rather than a compliment. The sign is clear, and it stays clear, and the difference between the traveler who learns something from a ruin and the traveler who sees a pile of stones lies entirely in whether he was willing to be told anything. The next verse turns to another community that was given the same opportunity.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:36

> And to the people of Midian ˹We sent˺ their brother Shu’aib. He said, “O my people! Worship Allah, and hope for ˹the reward of˺ the Last Day. And do not go about spreading corruption in the land.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Third Community Added to the Roll**

The chapter has moved from Abraham and Lot to a people living further north, and the pattern continues: a messenger is sent, he states his case in a sentence or two, and the outcome is reported. Midian is named in the Book as the people of Shuʿayb, and scholars connect their descent to Midian the son of Abraham, which supplies one reason the chapter places this account after the story of Abraham and his nephew. The sequence of the sūrah is not chronological in any ordinary sense; it is thematic. A community known for a particular kind of dishonesty is added to a list that includes towns destroyed for violence, and the reader is meant to see the common structure behind the different offences. The same warning, the same refusal, and the same end.

**"Their Brother," Not a Stranger**

The Book describes Shuʿayb as the brother of the people he addresses, and the expression recurs for the messengers of this period. It means that he came from among them, spoke their dialect, and knew their trade. The Book uses the word to close off an excuse: nobody could claim that a foreigner had misunderstood the local customs, and nobody could dismiss the warning as the intrusion of an outsider with no stake in the community. The phrase also makes the rejection heavier, since the man being mocked at the market was someone the merchants had grown up with. The sūrah has already shown the same dynamic in Abraham's town and in Lot's, and Midian repeats it.

The word also settles the question of a messenger's social position. Shuʿayb was not a foreign official bringing a policy for the town to adopt; he was a man whose family lived there and whose warning therefore implicated him in whatever the outcome would be, and the Book's use of the family term keeps that stake visible.

**Worship and Hope Joined in One Command**

The address opens with two obligations that belong together: worship God, and hope for the reward of the Last Day. The Book commonly attaches the Hereafter to worship because the two stand or fall as a pair. A person who believes that this life is all there is has no reason to accept a loss today for a return tomorrow, and the expectation of the Last Day is what makes honest dealing in a dishonest market a rational course. The midianites were being asked, in other words, to change the horizon against which they measured a transaction. The sentence does not mention Paradise or punishment in detail; it names hope as the motive that would make the rest of the instruction practicable.

**Corruption, the Same Word From Lot's Prayer**

The third command returns to the term the sūrah used when Lot asked for help against a people of corruption. The Book applies the word to the specific malpractice of Midian, and the fuller version of Shuʿayb's warning states the charge in economic language: give just measure and weight, do not defraud people of their property, and do not spread corruption in the land after it has been set in order (7:85). The pairing is deliberate. Fraud in the scales is not treated as a business irregularity but as damage to the land itself, because a market that cannot be trusted corrodes everything that depends on it. The sūrah is showing the reader how wide its central term reaches.

The word's appearance in both stories also keeps the sūrah's charges connected. What Lot called corruption in the conduct of his townspeople is the same disorder the Book finds in fraudulent scales, and the shared vocabulary ties the two offences together as failures of one covenant rather than as separate categories of crime.

**Why Commerce Is a Religious Subject**

A reader may find it remarkable that a chapter about trial and persecution stops to warn a merchants' town about weights. The Book makes no apology for it. Trade is where a community's honesty becomes visible, and the damage caused by a rigged scale is measured in the households that were shorted by it, which is why the Book assigns the offence consequences in both this life and the next. Midian's crime lacked the drama of Sodom's, and the Book still treats it as corruption serious enough to end a civilization. The warning stands in the record beside the warnings given to murderers and idolaters, and the placement tells the reader what the Book thinks of a community that cheats the people who buy from it.

The Book never treats weights and measures as a private matter for merchants to settle among themselves, and that refusal is deliberate. A community's food supply and the trust between its traders are conditions others depend on, so fraud in the market is described in the same terms as the denial of God, which the surrounding verses treat as the first of the people's offences.

**What Is Meant by a Land Set in Order**

The fuller warning speaks of corruption committed after the land had been set in order, a phrase that presupposes a settled condition with laws, markets, and expectations that people could rely on. The Book's picture of a healthy society is not a place of perfect virtue but a place where the ordinary arrangements work and the weak are not systematically robbed. Midian had disrupted that condition from within, using the machinery the society had built to extract from the people inside it. The verb the sūrah uses for the final destruction of such communities is used everywhere for the collapse of a whole arrangement, and the account that follows in this chapter will report the end of Midian in a single line.

The phrase describes an outcome that depends on conduct rather than on geography. A town with honest scales, settled disputes, and secure roads is in order because of what its people do, and the command to Midian is therefore framed as a repair job rather than as a conquest or a relocation.

**A Messenger Who Knew Their Business**

The Book sends prophets out of the communities they address, and in Shuʿayb's case the messenger understood the trade he was rebuking. The fuller account preserves his warning with the reason attached: he tells the people of Midian that he sees them in prosperity and that he fears for them an overwhelming day, then names the short measure as the thing he fears it for (11:84). The sentence combines affection with alarm, which is a register the Book uses for messengers speaking about their own towns. Shuʿayb was not denouncing an abstraction called fraud; he was describing the practices of shops he had grown up with and the households that would be ruined by what was coming, and the warning keeps both facts in view at once.

**The Trade the Book Is Willing to Argue About**

The specifics of Shuʿayb's case are preserved in other chapters, and they show how far the Book is prepared to go into the details of commerce. He tells his people to give full measure and weigh with justice, and warns them not to defraud people of their property or spread corruption in the land (7:85). Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ repeats the instruction twice, ordering full measure and no loss to others, and forbidding fraud and corruption in the same breath (26:181; 26:183). The Book attaches these rules to the creed rather than to a code of trading practice, and it treats a short weight as an act of misguidance rather than a civil matter. Midian was warned about the scale, and the warning carries the same weight as a warning about worship, which is the measure of how seriously the Book regards a market that cheats its customers.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:37

> But they rejected him, so an ˹overwhelming˺ earthquake struck them and they fell lifeless in their homes.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verdict Stated in One Clause**

The whole response of Midian to its messenger is compressed into the words "they rejected him," and the report of their end follows immediately. The Book does this repeatedly in this chapter, and the compression is a judgment in itself. No extended dialogue is preserved, no argument is presented, and no excuse is offered on the town's behalf; the record shows only the decision and its consequence. The reader who wants the details of Shuʿayb's preaching has the fuller accounts in other chapters, where he argues about weights and measures and warns about a punishment that will fall on a lying marketplace (7:85; 11:85). What the sūrah keeps is the essential move, because the chapter's subject is not the content of one prophet's sermon but the pattern that repeats across the nations.

What the clause leaves out is as telling as what it contains. There is no inventory of the town's possessions, no description of its buildings, and no account of survivors; the report is narrowed to a verdict and its execution, and the sūrah's habit of ending these narratives at the moment of seizure keeps the reader's mind on the cause.

**The Same Sentence Given to Two Nations**

The description of this destruction is identical in wording to the account of the Thamūd, who were struck down after rejecting Ṣāliḥ and were found lifeless in their dwellings (7:91). Two nations, two messengers, two distinct crimes, one sentence. The Book's use of the same formula for both fates is a way of teaching the reader that the differences between the offences do not matter at the level of consequence. Fraud in the scales and the killing of a prophet's camel are both breaches of the same covenant, and both end with a community lying dead in the houses it built. The repetition across chapters makes the point in a way that a fresh description would not, since a reader who recognizes the words recognizes the rule.

**Found Lifeless Where They Lived**

The phrase is worth slowing down for. The people were not caught at the city's edge or in the market; they were found in their homes, in the private places that feel most secure. Commentators describe the posture the word suggests — bodies prostrate or rigid — and note the humiliation implied in a death that arrives in the position of the utterly helpless. The Book's catastrophe narratives frequently locate the end indoors, where the ordinary assumption of safety rests, because the point is not that the victims were caught unprepared in some exposed place. It is that the ground beneath a comfortable house belongs to its owner no less than the ground beneath a wilderness.

The location also makes the interruption audible. These were people who expected to spend that day trading, arguing, and eating in rooms they had built, and the sentence finds them at home rather than on a field of battle, which is where the Book often places its scenes of sudden judgment.

**The Ground Itself as the Instrument**

An earthquake is a peculiar choice for a merchant people, and the mismatch carries a lesson for a town that measured its security in warehouses and roads. The Midianites depended on settled land, caravans, and stable thoroughfares; their prosperity was possible only because the earth held still under their feet. What ends them is the failure of that assumption. Sūrah Sabaʾ asks whether the people have not seen everything that surrounds them of the heavens and the earth, and whether God could not cause the earth to swallow them or drop deadly fragments of the sky upon them, adding that in this is a sign for every servant who turns to God (34:9). The earth's steadiness is a gift that is spent without being noticed.

**Whether the Messenger Survived**

The sūrah does not say what became of Shuʿayb, and the other accounts fill the gap: the Book elsewhere reports the destruction of the people and the deliverance of their prophet with those who believed with him. The pattern is now familiar from the chapter's first narratives. Noah, Abraham, and Lot were all taken out of the ruin that came upon their people, and the reader is meant to expect the same here. This is one of the ways the sūrah has been consoling its first audience: across every nation it has surveyed, the messenger survives his rejection, and the believing remnant survives with him, even when the remnant is one household. The Book's pattern is consistent enough that its audience could rely on it, and the reliance is the point: a person who delivers the message is not required to perish with the people who refuse it, and the community that follows him is not abandoned to share their end. What the sūrah has been establishing through these repeated accounts is that being on the right side of a rejected message is survivable, and that the survivors are gathered out before the matter is closed.

**Why the Same Ending Is Reported Again and Again**

By this point in the chapter, the reader has seen four communities reduced, and the accumulation is the argument. A single ruined nation might be a coincidence of history; a list of them, each with a messenger, a rejection, and a judgment, establishes a rule. The Book is preparing its audience to see their own situation inside that sequence, and it holds back nothing about the outcomes. The next verses add two more Arabian tribes and a trio of Egyptian names, and only after the roll is complete does the sūrah state its general verdict, that Allah did not wrong these peoples but that they wronged themselves.

The recurrence also confirms that the sūrah is not recounting local history for its own sake. Each of these towns sits at a different distance from the audience in time and place, and the repetition of one ending across all of them is what turns a series of anecdotes into a rule each hearer must apply to himself.

**Nobody Was Destroyed Before Being Told**

The Book states its rule about destruction in terms that leave no room for the tragedy of the uninformed: God would never destroy a society for its wrongdoing while its people are unaware of the truth (6:131), and He would never destroy a society until He had sent a messenger to its capital, reciting His revelations to its people (28:59). The second passage adds the condition that the destruction follows on persistence in wrongdoing. Midian therefore fits the rule rather than escaping it: a messenger had lived among them, they had heard the warning about their scales, and the earthquake is presented as the end of a process that began with instruction. The sentence reports a seizure, but the Book's wider statements show what stood between the warning and the tremor.

Stating the rule this way also explains why the Book's messengers spend so long arguing. The warning is not a formality that precedes an inevitable sentence; it is the condition on which the sentence is held back, and the length of Noah's mission, the patience shown to Pharaoh, and the years Lot spent among his neighbours are all applications of the same policy.

**The Identical Sentence in Sūrah al-Aʿrāf**

Sūrah al-Aʿrāf gives the Midianite destruction in exactly the words this verse uses, noting that an overwhelming earthquake seized them and that they were found lifeless in their dwellings (7:91). Sūrah Hūd gives the same end for Thamūd and Midian in adjacent passages, which is why commentators treat the two destructions as a matched pair. The Book's habit of preserving one formula for these events means that a reader who has learned the pattern can predict the ending of any story he encounters, and the predictability is the point. The chapter is not narrating tragedy for its own sake; it is teaching a rule that its audience is expected to apply to itself.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:38

> And the people of ’Ȃd and Thamûd ˹met a similar fate˺, which must be clear to you ˹Meccans˺ from their ruins. Satan made their ˹evil˺ deeds appealing to them, hindering them from the ˹Right˺ Way, although they were capable of reasoning.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Tribes the Audience Had Seen For Themselves**

The sūrah now names two Arabian peoples rather than distant nations, and it addresses the Meccans directly about them. ʿĀd and Thamūd were ancient tribes of the peninsula, sent the prophets Hūd and Ṣāliḥ respectively, and their ruined settlements lay along the caravan routes the audience travelled for trade. The Book's argument changes register here. Abraham, Lot, and Shuʿayb belonged to lands the Meccans had heard of; ʿĀd and Thamūd belonged to places they had personally ridden through, and the chapter says so in the plainest terms by telling them that their fate is evident from their dwellings. Evidence that can be visited is being placed in front of people who had already visited it without looking.

The choice of these two peoples also avoided an objection the audience could have raised. Their remains lay within reach of the caravan routes, so a Meccan trader could look at what was left of them, and a messenger arguing from a distant legend would have invited a very different response.

**Ruins as an Argument From Geography**

The Book repeatedly converts travel into evidence and treats the failure to draw conclusions from landscape as a form of blindness. The same ruins are addressed in another chapter, where the Meccans are told that they pass by the remains of the destroyed towns day and night and are asked whether they will not then understand (37:137–138). The question presumes that the physical remains of a civilization ought to be enough to force the inquiry, since a settlement does not empty itself. The sūrah's audience had a working explanation available for the ruins they saw — old places, abandoned for reasons of trade or water — and the Book's challenge is that the explanation leaves out the one fact the ruins were preserved to convey.

The Book treats the landscape as part of its evidence rather than as scenery. Empty settlements, abandoned wells, and fallen walls appear in its arguments repeatedly, and the sūrah's audience travelled past them for a living, which is why the verse can present the ruins as material that was already familiar.

**Satan Made Their Deeds Look Fair**

The cause of the collapse is named in a clause that has nothing to do with intelligence: Satan made their deeds attractive to them. The commentators explain that this means the people came to regard their own disbelief and their rejection of the messengers as marks of their superiority, as though holding out against a prophet were a sign of discernment. The Book uses the same phrase about several groups of deniers, and the mechanism is always the same: the sin is not presented as a temptation to pleasure but as an assumption of personal excellence. A community that has been persuaded that its stubbornness is wisdom has lost the ability to hear a warning, and no argument can reach it because the argument itself has been classified as beneath its dignity.

**The Road and the Departure From It**

The verse describes the effect of the deception as being turned aside from the way, and it uses the language of a path. The image is one of the Book's most consistent: guidance is a road, error is a departure from it, and the danger is not a wall across the road but a slow drift away from its edge. The verb can also carry the sense of blocking or barring, which makes the metaphor even harsher, since it suggests an obstruction placed deliberately. Between the two senses the reader gets a complete picture of the process: the travellers were flattered into leaving the road, and the road was then closed behind them.

Describing the ruin as a departure from a road also defines what the two tribes lost. They had the faculties, the messengers, and the settled land; what they lacked was a direction, and the Book's image for their condition is not weakness but a walk away from something they could see.

**"Although They Were Capable of Reasoning"**

The clause attached at the end is the verse's heaviest charge. Commentators read it in two ways, and both are admissible: that the people were intelligent and simply did not act in accordance with what they knew, or that they had clearly seen the truth through the messengers and therefore knew the nature of their error. Either way the word removes the defence of incapacity. The Book refuses to treat the destruction of these tribes as the fate of people too primitive to understand the message delivered to them. They had minds that worked and evidence that was explained, and what defeated them was not the quality of the reasoning but the attraction of a version of themselves they preferred.

**The Warning That Is Aimed at the Listener**

The direct address to the Meccans sharpens the whole passage, since the audience being invited to consider the ruins was on the same road with a messenger of its own. The sūrah has been assembling a list that ends with a general verdict, but it takes care before that verdict to put the reader inside the story. The same observation appears in the verse from Sūrah Sabaʾ that calls the surrounding heavens and earth a sign for every servant who turns to God (34:9). What separates a person from the ruins of ʿĀd is not a different nature and not a different level of intelligence; it is whether he draws the conclusion available to him while he still has time to act on it.

The warning also reaches a reader with no ruins near his house. The Book leaves the story in place so that the conduct behind the destruction can be recognized wherever it appears, and the sūrah has spent many verses describing that conduct in the audience's own town.

**The Ruins That Sat on the Trade Route**

The Book does not leave the remains of these peoples to be discovered by archaeologists; it locates them on the roads its audience used. The ruins of the destroyed tribe are described as still lying along a known route (15:76), and the Book says of the cities that were showered with stones that the Meccans had certainly passed by them and asks whether they had not seen the ruins, adding that those travelers did not expect to be resurrected (25:40). The argument of this verse and the argument of those passages are one argument. The evidence was not buried in a distant land or hidden in an archive; it stood beside the caravan tracks in the form of empty settlements, and the only step the Book required was the step of asking why they were empty.

**The Deceiver Does Not Excuse the Deceived**

The mention of Satan in this verse raises a question the Book answers elsewhere with consistency. If a deceiver made the deeds attractive, does the deception carry the responsibility for the outcome? The Book never treats it that way. Satan is described as an agent who invites and adorns, and the people who follow him are described as having been capable of reasoning, which is exactly the phrase this verse attaches to the tribes that fell. The Book's position is that a suggestion is not a compulsion, and that the presence of a tempter accounts for the attractiveness of a course without accounting for the decision to take it. The Meccans addressed in this verse were hearing the same kind of allurement in their own marketplaces, and the sentence gives them the tool to recognize it: the deeds that look like excellence are often the ones doing the work of an enemy.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:39

> ˹We˺ also ˹destroyed˺ Korah, Pharaoh, and Hamân. Indeed, Moses had come to them with clear proofs, but they behaved arrogantly in the land. Yet they could not escape ˹Us˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Names From a Single Story**

The chapter adds a trio drawn from the Egyptian narrative: Korah, whose wealth the Book describes at length; Pharaoh, whose claim to divinity is recorded in several chapters; and Hāmān, the official who executed the king's projects. The three represent different kinds of power rather than three unrelated criminals. One held money, one held the throne, and one ran the apparatus that turned royal ambition into buildings and persecutions. By naming them together the sūrah broadens its indictment beyond the tribes and merchant towns it has surveyed, and it shows the reader that the pattern being established is not a feature of any particular social order. Nomadic confederations, trading cities, and an imperial state all arrive at the same verdict.

The three names are taken from different chapters of one national history and placed in a single sentence, which is the Book's way of showing that the pattern outlasted any single ruler. A reader who knows how far apart these figures stand in time will see the verse treating them as one case with three exhibits.

**Korah and the Wealth That Went Into the Ground**

The Book gives Korah's story in another chapter, where he is a man of enormous fortune who is reminded that his wealth is a test and answers by claiming it was given to him for his own merit. His end is reported in a single sentence: the earth swallowed him along with his house, and there was no one to help him against God, nor could he help himself (28:81). The detail of the house matters because it ties his fate to the wealth it contained. What he had accumulated as security became the wreckage in which he was buried, and the assets that made him formidable in the city were irrelevant in the space of an instant. The Book's verdict on his prosperity is not that it was evil; it is that he mistook it for protection.

The wealth of Korah is described elsewhere as belonging to a man who attributed his fortune to his own knowledge, and the ground's treatment of him answers that claim directly. The verse under discussion keeps the burial at the level of a fact, since the sūrah's interest lies in the collapse rather than in the argument that preceded it.

**Pharaoh's Tower and Hāmān's Labour**

The role assigned to Hāmān in the Book is precise. He is the official ordered to light a fire and build a tower so that Pharaoh could look upon the God of Moses and, in the fuller accounts, so that he could be proclaimed a deity to the public (28:38). The project is a good illustration of the problem the chapter has been describing. Imperial power in the Book is not merely cruel; it is absurd, spending the labour of a nation to prove a claim that no structure can support. Pharaoh's real objection to Moses was never about evidence, since the proofs were displayed before him, and the tower is what his position produced when evidence failed: an architectural argument, built by the man standing beside him in this verse. The Book's portrait of the court is completed by the fact that the project required the labour of a nation and produced nothing usable. It is one of the few places in the scripture where a government's most formidable undertaking is described as an exercise in vanity, and the reader is meant to notice how much of the state's power was spent on a claim that the state could not make true.

**Clear Proofs Answered With Arrogance**

The sentence says the proofs came first and arrogance followed, and the sequence is the point of the accusation. The Book's vocabulary for arrogance describes more than a bad manner; it names the refusal of a person to accept a truth he knows, in order to preserve his standing. Pharaoh had seen the signs and knew the position he was occupying; his response was to hold to it anyway. The sūrah has already shown the same reflex in the towns it surveyed, where the people who could reason decided not to, and the Egyptian court gives the pattern its political form. A ruler surrounded by his own monument does not need to be convinced of anything in order to keep refusing it.

The proof was not a puzzle the audience failed to solve; it was a demonstration they understood and dismissed, and the Book's accounts of these figures describe men who knew what they were refusing. The charge concerns attitude rather than information, and the following sentence treats the refusal itself as the reason for what came next.

**"They Could Not Escape Us"**

The closing clause uses the exact word the sūrah employed earlier when it told its audience that no one escapes God on earth or in heaven (29:22). The repetition ties the two passages together and closes the distance between the imperious and the ordinary. A ruler with a treasury, an army, and a tower is in the same position as any other creature before the decree, and his apparatus cannot delay it by a day. The Book's reports of these regimes are consistently unimpressed by their scale, and the phrase reminds the reader that the empires that survive in the record do so only as material for the argument. The next verse gathers the verdicts of the whole chapter into a single statement.

The phrase closes the case against all three at once. None of them escaped, and the sentence draws no distinction between the man with the treasure, the man with the army, and the man with the building contract, which leaves the reader facing a limit that no kind of power can negotiate.

**Why the List Keeps Widening**

The sūrah has moved from a corrupt town, to a fraudulent market, to two Arabian tribes, to a king and his officials, and it has reported the same outcome for each. This is the technique of the whole passage: the reader is shown that the pattern holds across every type of community that has ever existed, and no listener can claim that his own society falls outside the categories. The Meccan audience that heard the chapter had merchants, tribesmen, and rulers of its own, and the list gives each of its constituencies a case to consider. What remains, after all the names have been gathered, is the single verdict with which the passage closes. The order of the list is itself instructive. It begins with the community that killed its prophet's credibility by force, moves to a market that robbed its customers, passes through two tribes that treated their messengers with contempt, and ends at a throne that tried to answer revelation with architecture. Every path away from the message is represented, and each one terminates in the same place.

**Korah's End Retold in Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ**

Sūrah al-Qaṣaṣ reports Korah's destruction with a detail that belongs beside this verse: the earth swallowed him and his home, and the verse notes that those who had envied his position the day before were struck the next day by the realization that God enlarges provision for whomever He wills or withholds it (28:81–82). The observation attaches the story directly to the sūrah's later theme about provision, and it shows that the punishment did its work on the survivors as well. Korah's wealth had made him a model to be envied, and his end made him a warning in the same streets, which is the pattern the whole chapter follows: the ruin becomes a lesson for those still in a position to reconsider.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:40

> So We seized each ˹people˺ for their sin: against some of them We sent a storm of stones, some were overtaken by a ˹mighty˺ blast, some We caused the earth to swallow, and some We drowned. Allah would not have wronged them, but it was they who wronged themselves.

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Verdict, Four Methods**

The verse gathers the whole catalogue of destroyed nations into a single sentence and reports four methods of seizure. The variation is the striking feature. No two communities were ended in exactly the same way, and yet each is described as having been taken for its own sin. The Book has been building to this summary for many verses, and its purpose is to prevent the reader from attaching the lesson to a particular disaster. Fire from the sky, a cry that overwhelms, the ground opening, and water closing over a host are different events, but they are described here as one act performed by one authority, distributed according to circumstances that the verse says are known.

The list also shows that the Book does not treat the manner of destruction as the point of the account. What the four cases share is the verdict, and the variety of instruments—water, wind, sound, and a swallowing earth—keeps a reader from attaching the lesson to any one of them.

**Which People Met Which End**

The identifications are set out by the commentators. The storm of stones belongs to the people of Lot, whose end is recorded in the sūrah's own narrative and in the parallel accounts of the cities turned upside down. The blast, rendered elsewhere as a cry or a shout, is assigned to the tribes of Thamūd and Midian, both of whom the Book describes as being found lifeless in their homes after the sound came. The opening of the earth is Korah's end, swallowed with his house. The drowning belongs to the people of Noah and to Pharaoh's army, whose destruction in the sea is one of the Book's most familiar scenes. Each identification fits the pattern the sūrah has laid out, and the four together cover the entire list of communities named in the chapter.

**The Elements as Instruments of Judgment**

Commentators observe that the four methods fall out across the traditional elements: water in the drowning, earth in the swallowing, air in the wind that carried the stones, and fire in the blast understood as lightning or as the thunderbolt that accompanied it. The observation is not a claim about alchemy; it names the point the summary is making. Every part of the created order stands available to its maker as an instrument, and the communities that trusted in the stability of that order are ended by it. A scene this comprehensive leaves no place for a person to imagine that some corner of the world is exempt, and the sūrah has already told its audience in plain words that there is no escape on earth or in heaven. The four methods also dispel any notion that the judgments of the past were regional accidents. A merchant in a walled town, a nomad in open country, a farmer on good soil, and an admiral at sea each find their own environment among the instruments listed, and none of the four environments affords protection.

**"Each for Their Sin"**

The distributive phrase keeps the account honest, since it rules out collective punishment in the sense that a careless reader might fear. Each people was seized for what it had done, and the differences in their crimes correspond to differences in their reports. The Book never treats these destructions as the accidents of geography or as the misfortunes of a region that happened to be in the way of a flood. The word used for the cause covers error and wrongdoing alike, and it is applied to communities that had been warned by a messenger drawn from among them. What the sentence refuses to grant is the possibility that a people could be destroyed without a reason, and the reason is located in their own conduct.

The phrase assigns responsibility one by one rather than in a lump. Nations are discussed as bodies in the historical passages of the sūrah, while the sentence about judgment returns to the individual, and the shift matters for a reader tempted to hide inside a crowd, whether for blame or for safety.

**The Disclaimer That Closes the Ledger**

The final clause states the principle that the entire chapter has been demonstrating: God did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves. The formula recurs throughout the Book whenever the destruction of a nation is described, and the repetition is deliberate. It protects the reader from the portrait of a deity who acts on appetite, and it protects the record from the accusation that the punishments were disproportionate. The wrong in the sentence is reflexive, which is the Book's consistent understanding of sin: it damages the one who commits it before it damages anyone else, and the communities in this chapter are presented as having completed that damage on a scale that ended their existence. The verse's wording also denies any gap between the offence and the answer: the seizure is described as following the sin directly, with no intervening accident to blame.

**The Refrain That Repeats Across the Book**

The Book attaches the same disclaimer to nearly every account of national destruction, and its recurrence is a deliberate protection of the reader's understanding of God. The same sentence closes the story of the people in the wilderness, who were told that the evildoers did not wrong God but wronged themselves (2:57), and it appears in the punishment of the tribes and the overturned cities, where the Book states that God would have never wronged them but that it was they who wronged themselves (9:70). Five nations, five methods, one explanation, repeated in identical words. The repetition is the Book's way of ensuring that no reader draws the conclusion the disbelievers were accused of drawing elsewhere in the sūrah — that the disaster was arbitrary.

The repetition also serves a teaching purpose, since a community that hears the same clause at the end of story after story will eventually attach it to its own situation. The Book's habit of repeating a verdict in identical words makes the lesson portable, and this sūrah uses the technique at the point where the list of nations finally stops.

**What the Summary Does for the Chapter**

With this verse the sūrah closes the long historical survey that began with the story of Abraham and Lot. The verdict is stated as a general rule, and the reader is now in possession of the principle that the chapter's opening verses asserted about the testing of believers. The two halves of the sūrah fit together: the first half said that claims of faith would be examined, and the second has shown what happened to the communities that failed the examination. What follows in the chapter turns from history to comparison, and the sūrah chooses an image from the animal world to make its next point about where people place their trust. The transition is the chapter's way of applying the lesson. Having established that the consequences are real, the Book asks its readers to examine what they are relying on, and it offers them a picture so ordinary that no one can claim to have missed the point. The historical survey supplied the evidence; the comparison that follows supplies the test of whether a reader has understood the evidence or merely heard it.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:41

> The parable of those who take protectors other than Allah is that of a spider spinning a shelter. And the flimsiest of all shelters is certainly that of a spider, if only they knew.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shelter Built by the Thing That Needs It**

The image is exact, and its force lies in a detail the reader supplies without being told. The spider does not find its house; it spins one out of its own body. Everything the web is made of comes from the maker, and the structure offers no protection against anything heavier than itself. Applied to the people described in the verse, the picture says that the protectors they have chosen are not external powers they have enlisted but arrangements they have produced. The Book's criticism in this chapter has been consistent about that: idols that cannot provide, patrons who will disown their clients, wealth that sinks with its owner. What all of them share is that the security is manufactured by the person who feels secure.

**What the Sūrah Means by a Protector**

The term rendered as protector names the person or power to whom one attaches oneself for support, and the Book uses it across a wide range of commitments. It criticizes those who take other human beings as their patrons, those who take devils as allies, and those who take idols as their reliance. In the Book's vocabulary the office belongs to God alone, while in the human realm it belongs to the Prophet and to the community of believers who stand together. The sentence therefore does not only condemn those who bow to statues. It addresses anyone who has arranged his safety around a source that is not God, whether that source is a state, a patron, a fortune, or a reputation, and it asks what such a shelter is actually made of.

**Why the Image Is a House Rather Than a Wall**

The Book chose the picture of a dwelling rather than a weapon, and the choice carries its own argument. A house is what a person builds to be at rest, and what he trusts to be there when he returns. The web is genuinely a house by that definition: it is spun to catch, it shelters the spider, and it is precisely where the spider lives. The problem is not that the shelter is fake but that it is unequal to the pressures that will arrive. The sūrah has just spent many verses reporting those pressures, and the reader who has followed the fates of five nations knows what the weather eventually does to a structure of that kind.

A wall is judged by what it can repel; a house is judged by what it can hold. The comparison in the verse therefore tests the idolatrous arrangement on the second question rather than the first. What the town had built was a religion that organized its calendar, its trade, and its standing, and the assertion of the verse is that none of it would hold up the thing it was meant to support.

**"If Only They Knew"**

The closing words locate the entire error in knowledge, and they repeat a form the sūrah has already used. The sentence does not say the people are insincere or that they deliberately chose a failing protection; it says they do not know what their protection is worth. That is the same diagnosis the chapter offered at its opening, when it asked whether people suppose their claim of faith will not be tested, and the same one Lot's town received when it answered a warning with mockery. In each case the difficulty is not malice but a mistaken assessment of the material a person is standing on, and the Book's remedy is always to state the assessment plainly.

The clause implies that the knowledge in question was already within reach, since a people cannot be told that they would change course if they knew something nobody had offered to explain. What it exposes is a gap between information and attention. The town had heard the case and had not taken it up, and the short form of the sentence leaves the reader with the question of which of the two he is short of.

**The Wider Use of the Image in the Book**

The spider's web became a standard image in the Book's later interpretation for everything a person builds without God at the centre: religious systems invented by human beings, acts of worship performed for the approval of an audience, and ideologies that promise what they cannot deliver. The generalization is not a stretch, since the Book states the underlying principle elsewhere when it says that the deeds of those who disbelieve in their Lord are like ashes scattered by the wind, with no power over anything they have earned (14:18), and it says of certain deeds that they have come to nothing (2:217; 3:22; 5:53). What the image adds is the reason: the structure was never designed to bear the load.

The same concern for durability runs through the Book's other statements about work that is not grounded in what is true, and those statements ask one question of every enterprise a person undertakes: what will it be worth once the conditions that made it convenient have ended? Read beside them, the spider's web is a member of a family rather than a curiosity, and it is the member chosen for a chapter addressed to people who had built their whole standing on an inherited cult.

**What the Parable Does Not Say**

The verse does not claim that people who take false protectors gain nothing in this world. The spider's web does catch what lands in it, and the sūrah's own history has shown patrons, markets, and empires functioning for a long time. What the image denies is durability, not effectiveness. That distinction is what makes the parable intelligible to a merchant or a tribal leader in the audience, since neither would be moved by the claim that his arrangements accomplish nothing; both would be struck by the claim that the arrangements cannot survive the event they were built to survive. The next verse states what the people in the parable are actually calling upon, and the answer it gives is short enough to be memorized.

Nor does the verse deny that the arrangement delivered anything in this life. Webbing does keep off some weather, and the cult of the town did give its members trade, standing, and a shared calendar. The denial is narrower and more serious: it concerns what the arrangement could do on the day the sūrah keeps returning to, and the sentence is aimed at that day alone.

**What the Spider's House Holds Up**

A further point in the image rewards attention: the web is not only fragile, it is also the means by which the spider obtains what it lives on. The shelter and the livelihood are one structure, and both depend on the same threads. Applied to the passage, this says that the false protector does not merely fail to shield the person relying on it; it also determines how he feeds himself. A merchant who trusted in a market built on cheating had his income and his security in one arrangement, and when the arrangement failed, he lost both at once. The Book's critiques of misplaced reliance consistently show this doubling, and the image of the web catches it in a single object. It also explains why the sūrah has spent so much of its length on the fate of the arrangements themselves rather than on the sincerity of the people who made them, since the question the image raises is not whether a person trusts his shelter but what the shelter is made of.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:42

> Allah surely knows that whatever ˹gods˺ they invoke besides Him are ˹simply˺ nothing. For He is the Almighty, AllWise.

**Expanded Commentary**

The complaint against the town's devotion is put in terms of knowledge rather than of power, which is a more damaging charge. A weak ally who has been told what is needed can still be useful, whereas a thing that cannot be informed of the request cannot respond to it at all. Every act of worship is a claim to have been heard, and the verse removes that claim by naming the one party in the relationship who is described as knowing everything. The Book's argument against the idols therefore does not depend on what they could do; it depends on what they were.

**The Verse States an Empty Set**

The sentence names the objects of invocation and then states outright that they are nothing. The reader who has followed the chapter recognizes the claim, since the sūrah has already described idols that cannot provide, patrons who will disown their dependants, and protectors who cannot protect. What is new here is the form of the statement: the Book is not saying that the gods of the disbelievers are weak, inferior, or subordinate. It reports that they are nothing at all, while at the same time acknowledging that people invoke them and organize their lives around them. The verse's construction holds both facts, since the first half is about what people do and the second half is about what actually exists. The God who is addressed knows the difference even when the worshippers do not.

**Why the Verse Begins With Knowledge**

The sentence opens with an assertion about what God knows rather than an assertion about what the gods are, and the ordering is deliberate. The people addressed have their own account of the matter, and it is not a philosophical one; they call upon these beings, they feel their needs met by them, and they conclude that something is there. The verse does not argue with that experience. It places the judgement with the one who knows the situation entirely, and it leaves the audience to consider which of the two assessments is the one that will hold. The Book uses this structure repeatedly in its arguments about idolatry, and it appears again in the same sūrah when it tells the reader that God knows what is in the breasts of all beings.

The Book leaves the point where it stands rather than drawing out the consequence. Delay turns the same sentence into a warning to those who live in safety and into a comfort to anyone waiting for relief, and both readings are available from one line of text. What the verse rules out is the assumption that the arrangement is neutral: a power that holds everything and knows everything is either a reason to be careful or a reason to be patient, and the reader is left to decide which.

**The Idols That Cannot Hear**

Other chapters supply the reasoning that stands behind this brief verdict. Abraham asks his father why he worships what can neither hear nor see nor benefit him at all (19:42), and the Book tells the Prophet to ask the disbelievers how they can worship besides God those who can neither harm nor benefit them, when God alone is the All-Hearing, the All-Knowing (5:76). The incapacity is total: no perception, no agency, no effect of any kind, whether for good or ill. The argument is not that the idols are weak deities but that they do not belong to the category of agents at all, and the verse's claim that they are nothing is the conclusion of that argument stated in its shortest form.

A person who relies on a thing that has no knowledge of him has placed his affairs in a relationship that runs in one direction only. The verse states the fact as part of an argument that runs through the whole sūrah: worship is a transaction with a party who hears, and the Book's practice of answering supplications is unintelligible without a hearer. When God is described as knowing everything, the sentence quietly removes the alternative, since the objects of the town's devotion were being asked for things they could not be informed of.

**The Names That Close the Sentence**

The verse ends with two divine names, and their placement answers the two possible objections to everything that has been said. The Almighty answers the question of power: the One who actually holds the matter is capable of doing what He wills, so the comparison between Him and the objects described is not a comparison within one scale. The All-Wise answers the question of judgement: nothing in His administration is arbitrary, so the exposure of the empty protectors is not an expression of rivalry but a statement of fact. The sūrah has paired these names before, and the pattern suggests that the Book's case against false reliance rests on authority and intelligence together rather than on force alone.

**What the Sentence Implies About the Worshippers**

If the objects invoked are nothing, the labour spent on them is spent on nothing, and the sentence quietly leaves that conclusion standing. The sūrah has already described the outcomes of such wasted effort in its account of the destroyed nations, and it will return to the theme in the verse that follows about parables and who understands them. The people in the parable are not described as victims of a deception imposed on them from outside; the verse simply records what they are doing and who is watching. That the observers of their conduct outlive their objects is the point on which the whole image turns.

The two names answer the two assumptions the sentence has just removed. Power closes the question of escape, since nothing in the heavens or the earth can be out of range of the one who holds it, and wisdom closes the question of fairness, since what the reckoning produces will not be arbitrary. Placing them at the end also keeps the verse from resting on sheer capacity. The sentence tells the reader what kind of power is being described, and the second name is what distinguishes it from the force of an object that happens to be larger than the objects around it.

**A Sentence That Reads Differently in Different Mouths**

The verse is addressed to a Prophet engaged in a dispute, and its confidence is that of a man who has been told the outcome. For the audience in Makkah it functioned as a warning, since it names the fate of everything they were relying on. Read by later communities, it keeps its edge, because the objects of invocation change while the structure of the mistake does not. What the Book consistently supplies in such passages is not an argument that the reader must win in a debate but a fact that the reader may verify in the ruins of the nations described earlier in the chapter. The next verse explains why so few readers act on what the parables show them.

**The Difference Between an Object and a Distraction**

One more distinction belongs to this verse, and it concerns the attitude of the people described. The Book does not say they deny God; the sūrah has already reported that the idolaters acknowledged Him as the maker and the provider while associating others with Him in worship. Their problem was not a missing creator but a crowded ledger, and the sentence's word "besides Him" identifies the specific error. What they call upon in addition to God turns out to be nothing, and the addition is what the verse is examining. It is a narrow charge, and it is sharper for being narrow: everything they have added to their devotion has no counterpart in existence, and the One they acknowledge beside their additions knows precisely that. The Book makes the same case elsewhere when it tells the Prophet to ask the idolaters whether they are informing God of something He does not know in the heavens or the earth (10:18), and when it invites them to invoke those they claim as divine and observe that they have no power to remove harm from them or transfer it to another (17:56). The additions were real only as activities, and activities without objects leave the ledger exactly where it was.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:43

> These are the parables We set forth for humanity, but none will understand them except the people of knowledge.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Parables Offered to Everyone, Understood by Few**

The sentence makes a distinction that this chapter has been circling since its opening: the material is public and the comprehension is not. The parables are set before humanity without restriction, and the same image which the spider's web provides is available to any hearer. What divides the audience is not access but recognition, and the Book locates the difference in knowledge rather than in intelligence or social standing. The chapter's first verses raised the same question about people who say they believe and expect to be left untroubled; the sūrah's method throughout has been to place the evidence in the open and let the response reveal what a person is.

The verse also presents the parables as public property. They are set before humanity rather than reserved for a school, which is why the Book's images draw on spiders, flies, mountains, and rain instead of technical vocabulary, and why a failure to profit from them is treated as a fault in the audience rather than a shortage in the material.

**What Knowledge Means in This Sentence**

The word rendered as knowledge in the Book's usage is not a quantity of information. It describes a person coming to know a thing as it actually is, and the Book attaches it to those who act on what they have understood. Al-Tustarī is reported to have said that God addresses the parables to humanity at large, because the things that testify to power point to the Powerful, and that only the elect comprehend them, so that knowledge is rare and comprehension of God rarer still. His point is that a person does not arrive at this knowledge by his own unaided reasoning, and that whoever imagines he has done so has been deluded. The sentence therefore describes a gift that requires the recipient's attention rather than an aptitude that some people simply possess.

**The Parable as a Test of the Hearer**

A comparison in the Book does more than illustrate; it sorts. The reader who accepts the image of the spider's web must also accept what it says about the reliability of his own arrangements, and that acceptance is where most readings stop. The same structure appears in the Book's wider use of comparisons, and the Book says of itself that God is not ashamed to set forth a parable of a gnat or something smaller, adding that He thereby leads many astray and guides many (2:26). The verse is one of the clearest statements in the Book that revelation is not neutral in its effects. Its content can be heard by anyone, and its function is to separate those who take the point from those who do not.

**Why the Chapter Needs This Statement**

By this point the sūrah has reported the fates of five communities, and a reader might reasonably ask why the evidence never persuaded them. The verse supplies the answer at the level of the individual: the same argument that ruins a person can also be declined, and the decline is not a failure of the argument. The Book makes the point about itself rather than about its audience, and it does so without condescension. In the following verse the sūrah turns from the closed archives of the past to the world that is still standing, and the sentence about knowledge prepares the reader for the jump in scale.

Stating the matter here also protects the chapter's earlier arguments from being read as a display of cleverness. The sūrah has argued with its opponents at length, and this sentence reminds the reader that those arguments were never expected to work by their own force, since comprehension of them is granted or withheld at a level below debate.

**The Adornment of a Full Heart**

The Book treats understanding as something that depends on the condition of the person who receives it, and it names the obstruction elsewhere with the language of rust and seals. It says of the hypocrites that their hearts were sealed so that they could not comprehend (63:3), and it warns that those who persist in rejecting a sign after it has been explained lose the capacity to benefit from signs in general. The classification in this verse is not arbitrary, then; the Book presents knowledge as a capacity that grows with use and atrophies with neglect. A person who has already decided what he will accept before he hears an argument is not in a position to understand the argument, whichever argument it is.

Knowledge described as adornment also implies visibility. A person whose understanding has settled into his character carries it in his dealings, his speech, and his patience, and a word connected with beauty suggests an effect meant to be noticed by others rather than kept private.

**Where the Sentence Points**

The verse closes the section on the spider's web and opens the way to the next subject. What follows shifts from the comparison of houses to the structure of the heavens and the earth, and from the question of who understands to the question of what the creation itself declares. The Book's habit is to end a passage about human failure with a reminder that the failure is not universal, and the phrase about the people of knowledge performs that function here. The reader is left with a plain question: what kind of hearer is he, given that the same sentence has just been set before everybody and understood by a few?

The sentence also sets the terms on which a reader should hold his own grasp of the material. A person who recognizes a parable's force does so by permission rather than by cleverness, and reading the verse this way keeps knowledge from becoming a possession its owner can take credit for.

**The Parable That Requires an Admission First**

Comprehension of a comparison in the Book usually demands something of the hearer before it yields anything. To understand the spider's web as a description of one's own arrangements, a person must first be willing to consider that his arrangements might be of that kind, and that willingness is not a feature of intelligence. The chapter has documented the refusal in every form it takes: the town that answered with violence, the market that dismissed a warning about its scales, the tribes that treated their messenger as an obstacle, and the king who commissioned a tower. None of them failed for want of information. Each had been told what it needed to know, and each declined to apply it to itself, which is why the verse locates understanding in a category of people rather than in a supply of facts.

**A Closing Statement That Keeps the Door Open**

The sentence is a conclusion, but it is also an invitation, and the two functions sit together without strain. A reader who finds himself outside the description has just been told exactly what distinguishes the people inside it, and nothing in the verse suggests the category is closed. The Book's warnings are consistently written so that the person hearing them can still move, and a statement about who understands a parable is one of the most useful things a person can encounter before he has decided what to do with what he has heard. The following verse turns directly to the creation itself, which is the Book's usual way of showing the reader that there is still something to look at.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:44

> Allah created the heavens and the earth for a purpose. Surely in this is a sign for the people of faith.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Creation Described as a Deliberate Act**

The Arabic phrase rendered as "for a purpose" is built on the word for truth, and it can be read in more than one way: that the heavens and the earth were created with truth as their foundation, or that they were created in order to manifest the truth. Commentators record both, and the Book states the same idea in several sūras with the same construction (39:5; 45:22; 46:3; 64:3). What the phrase excludes is the alternative the audience would have found in the intellectual air of the time: that the world is an accident, or a game, or the by-product of forces with no intention behind them. The verse asserts a maker, a purpose, and a design, and it does so in the same chapter that has just compared human arrangements to a spider's web.

The wording also rules out the notion that the world assembled itself or that its order is accidental. The Book's verbs for creation carry the sense of measuring and proportioning, which is why the argument from design in this verse can end in a call to reflect rather than in a demand for bare assent.

**The Purpose the Book Specifies**

Other passages supply what the purpose is, and they are consistent. God created the heavens and the earth for a purpose so that every soul may be paid back for what it has committed, and none will be wronged (45:22). Sūrah al-Rūm pairs the purpose with an appointed term and notes that most people deny the meeting with their Lord (30:8). The same chapter that reports the creation in truth describes the night and the day wrapped around one another and the sun and moon running to a fixed term (39:5). Taken together, the Book's statements make the creation purposeful in two directions at once: it exists for a term, and it exists so that what is done inside it can be accounted for. The verse's brevity assumes that its audience has heard those statements.

**Why the Sign Is Assigned to Believers**

The sūrah has assigned its signs to different audiences as it went along. The ruins of the destroyed towns were a lesson for people of understanding, and the instruction to travel and observe the origin of life was addressed to anyone who could look. This verse names believers instead, and the shift is deliberate. The created order announces a maker to anyone who studies it, but it announces a purpose only to a person who already expects one, and it announces accountability only to a person who accepts that he is accountable. Faith in this verse is not a precondition for the evidence but the faculty that reads the evidence as what it is. The Book's habit of tailoring the address to its audience is one of the reasons its arguments land differently on different readers.

**The Fragility That Belongs to the Sign**

The context supplies a further layer. The verse stands immediately after the parable of the spider's web and immediately before the command to recite and pray, and its subject is the created order that the web belongs to just as much as the spider does. The Book states elsewhere that everything is bound to perish except God Himself (28:88), and that the created world is not permanent but runs to a determined end. A sign that is itself passing away can still testify to something that does not pass, and the sūrah has been making exactly that point since its opening verses about the frailty of what people build. The heavens and the earth are a sign because they point past themselves.

The observation also keeps the reader from confusing the sign with its maker. What the heavens and the earth display is a design that could not have produced itself, and the Book's insistence on their dependency prevents the admiration they inspire from settling on the creation instead of on the one who made it.

**Parables Drawn From the Smallest Things**

The Book's willingness to argue from insects was a subject of comment even in its own time, and it defends the practice openly: God is not ashamed to set forth a parable of a gnat or something smaller, and by it He leaves many astray while guiding many (2:26). The spider is in that family, and so is every leaf and grain the Book invites people to consider. A reader who expects the scripture to reserve its evidence for grand phenomena has misread its method, which is to place the argument in things everyone can see and most people ignore. The verse's claim is that the same heavens and earth which host the argument are themselves part of it.

The Book's examples also share a quality that suits its audience: they can be checked without equipment. A spider's web, a gnat, a seed, and a rain cloud are all within reach of an ordinary observer, and the sūrah's arguments rely on that accessibility rather than on rare events or specialised knowledge.

**Looking Inward at One's Own Composition**

Sūrah al-Rūm adds the most personal version of this line of reasoning when it asks whether people have not reflected upon their own being, and then states that God created the heavens and the earth and everything in between for a purpose and an appointed term, adding that most people deny the meeting with their Lord (30:8). The appeal moves from the sky to the self, and the purpose named is again the meeting. This is where the sūrah's argument has been heading throughout: the creation is not only evidence of power but an arrangement with a schedule, and the reader is inside the arrangement. The verse under discussion calls that arrangement a sign for people of faith, and the sūrah's next command tells the believer what to do with what the sign shows him.

Looking inward and looking outward are joined in one argument. A person who examines the design of his own body finds the same proportioning he sees in the sky, and the Book often places the two witnesses side by side so that a reader who doubts one still has the other in front of him.

**The Heavens and the Earth as a Standing Argument**

The Book appeals to the sky and the soil constantly, and the reason is that they never stop being available. A traveler may pass a ruin once and forget it, and a community may dismiss the warning of a messenger it has known for years, but the heavens and the earth deliver their testimony every morning. The verse's placement in the sūrah follows a passage about invented protectors and a comparison to the spider's web, and the contrast it draws is between things that were made and things that were made up. What was made endures for its appointed term with a structure that works; what was invented collapses when it is leaned on. The sentence asks the reader to compare the two orders of object, and the comparison is available from his doorstep without a journey.

**A Reminder That Outlasts Every Generation**

Because the sign described here is the created order rather than a particular miracle, its audience is not limited to the people who witnessed the events of this chapter. Each generation inherits ruins it did not watch being ruined, but it inherits the heavens and the earth complete, and the verse attaches its appeal to that inheritance. The Book states the consequence of ignoring it in another passage that says most people are in denial of the meeting with their Lord even while they live inside a creation that runs to an appointed term (30:8). The sentence under discussion supplies the counterweight: for people who believe, the same arrangement that others take for granted works as a sign, and it keeps working as long as the arrangement stands.

## Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt 29:45

> Recite what has been revealed to you of the Book and establish prayer. Indeed, ˹genuine˺ prayer should deter ˹one˺ from indecency and wickedness. The remembrance of Allah is ˹an˺ even greater ˹deterrent˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you ˹all˺ do.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Commands Joined in One Sentence**

The instruction pairs recitation of the Book with the establishment of prayer, and the two are connected rather than merely listed. The Arabic verb used for prayer means to set it up, to make it stand, which describes an institution maintained deliberately rather than a feeling that comes and goes. Recitation is the traffic between the believer and revelation; prayer is the traffic between the believer and his Lord, and the verse gives both to a Prophet standing in a hostile city. The command arrives immediately after a passage about how things that are relied on can fail, and its placement suggests the answer the sūrah is developing: what holds a person up is not a structure he builds but a relationship he maintains.

The pairing also shows that the two activities belong to one programme. Recitation without prayer leaves a hearer informed but undisciplined, and prayer without recitation leaves him performing a form whose content he does not carry, so the Book asks for both in a single sentence.

**Prayer as a Deterrent, Stated as a Fact**

The verse does not merely recommend prayer; it states an effect. Genuine prayer should deter a person from indecency and from wrongdoing, and the Arabic construction carries the sense of a designed purpose. The criterion is unusually practical, since it gives the one praying a way to test what he is doing. A prayer that leaves the person exactly as he was, with the same conduct and the same appetites, is still obligatory and still valid in form, but the Book describes its intended function as unachieved. Commentators discuss at length whether the deterrent operates automatically through the act itself or through the understanding produced by what is recited in it, and the two explanations are complementary in practice: the rite is the container, and the meaning is the load.

**The River at the Door**

The Prophet described the effect of the five daily prayers in a comparison that has been preserved with its chain and its collection: he asked his companions whether a man with a river at his door who bathed in it five times a day would retain any dirt, and when they answered that no trace would remain, he told them that this is the likeness of the five prayers, with which God erases sins (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 528, narrated by Abū Hurayrah). The image is chosen for its ordinariness. Nobody would call a bath a heroic act, and the Prophet's point is that the benefit lies in the repetition of something unremarkable at a fixed address. What wears away a person's failings in this account is not intensity but return.

The image also makes the standard of purity ordinary rather than heroic. A person is asked to bathe in water he can already see, which means the instruction assumes a resource at hand rather than an exceptional effort, and the same assumption runs through the worship the sūrah asks of its audience.

**The Sanctity of the One Who Prays**

The Book states in another place that God accepts only from the reverent (5:27), and the tradition attributes to the Companion Ibn Masʿūd the observation that a person whose prayer does not restrain him from indecency and wrongdoing gains nothing from it but distance. [UNVERIFIED: the wording is widely reported in tafsīr works as a saying of Ibn Masʿūd, but no authentic chain with a collection number was confirmed in checking.] The canonical statement of the same principle is the verse itself, and it is enough for the reader's purposes. What the two formulations share is a refusal to separate the act from its fruit. The Book never treats worship as a transaction that can be completed in isolation from the life that contains it.

The protection is also stated in a way that does not depend on the worshipper's reputation. What restrains him is the act itself rather than the standing he has built up, and generations of readers have taken from the sentence the reassurance that a person's worship counts for something even when the rest of his record is uneven.

**The Remembrance That Is Greater**

The clause that follows has been read in two main ways, and both are preserved in the commentaries. One reading takes remembrance of God as the greatest of all acts of worship, greater than the rest of what a person does. The other takes the comparison differently: God's remembrance of the believer is greater than the believer's remembrance of Him, since the second is bound to time and place while the first is not. Both readings fit the Book's usage, and a third possibility is that the sentence deliberately holds them together. On any reading the verse refuses to leave prayer as an isolated duty and connects it to a state that continues outside it, which is why the Book elsewhere gives the command to establish prayer for the sake of remembrance (20:14).

**What Recitation Contributes**

The command to recite placed at the head of the verse gives the believer a supply of content for the inner life. The Book describes itself as a healing and a mercy for the believers (17:82) and as guidance for the conscious (2:2), and the meaning of what is recited is what makes the deterrence of the previous clause intelligible. A person who recites a passage about the fates of towns that answered their messengers with mockery carries the argument with him into the rest of his day.
